Update your preferences in Account Settings A man looks at the devastation in the aftermath of floods at Trishuli in Nepal's Nuwakot district on September 7, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The floods in Nepal and Tibet have been terrifying: some 1,386 people have died, and around 5,000 remain unaccounted for. The flood destroyed bridges, hydropower projects and entire towns. The scale of the loss is immense but in Nepal and Tibet, the contours of such a disaster are grimly familiar. The Himalayas have been regularly witnessing deadly flash floods. In July 2025, a flood originating in China crossed into Nepal through the Trishuli River basin and killed 19 people. Its dynamics were different, but also involved glacial meltwaters. A flood in the Indian state of Uttrakhand in 2021 killed more than 200 people, and another flood in the Indian state of Sikkim in 2023 killed 55 people. In 2022, the floods in Pakistan affected more than 33 million people, destroyed 1.7 million homes, and killed around 1,500. What is driving such recurring events? It would be easy to attribute the flood in Nepal and Tibet simply to climate breakdown. Nepal ranks sixth among the countries most vulnerable to climate risk, and Himalayan glaciers are melting faster as the planet warms. And climate change demonstrably contributed to other devastating events in the Hindu Kush Himalaya, including Pakistan’s 2022 floods. The precise role of climate change in the Aug. 26 flood in Nepal and Tibet is not yet clear. The leading theory about the flood is that warming climate means permafrost might be thawing faster, and can cause landslides when it seeps into cracks between rocks. World Weather Attribution (WWA), an international group of scientists that models the role of human-induced climate change in extreme weather events, is yet to report on the flood in Nepal and Tibet. Climate breakdown alone does not explain the scale of disasters in the Himalaya. Between June and August, India’s northeastern states of Assam, Nagaland, and Arunachal Pradesh experienced heavy rains and multiple flash floods. WWA found little evidence that these floods were a product of human-caused climate change. Instead, a host of other human choices helped create the conditions for disaster. Unplanned urbanization put more people in harm’s way. Deforestation left fewer trees to draw rainwater into the soil, and bare hillsides washed away more easily. Infrastructure disrupted natural drainage, leaving floodwaters nowhere to disperse. The death and destruction caused by the flood in Nepal reflects a similar combination of factors. Keshab Kumar Sharma, a former secretary of Nepal’s infrastructure ministry, argued that unregulated industries, including riverbed mining and the dumping of excavated soil into rivers, had raised riverbeds and made the river channels more prone to flooding. The worst devastation occurred in Nepal’s Rasuwa district, on the border with China. Yet Rasuwa has also been one of the country’s most rapidly developing regions, with new trade routes, border facilities, and Chinese-backed hydropower projects. The interplay of water, infrastructure, and danger drives home the central lesson of disaster risk reduction: there is no such thing as a ‘natural’ disaster. A natural hazard becomes a disaster only when people are placed in its path. Had the floodwaters surged over uninhabited riverbanks where there were no people, roads, or farmland, the event would not have been called a disaster. For the damage to be so profound, the people in the flood’s path must also have been vulnerable: their homes, livelihoods, and sources of income exposed to destruction. Then there is the question of tourism. The narrow valleys and mountain gorges around Rasuwa district, which are home to several hydropower projects, are also major tourist destinations. The disappearance of hundreds foreign tourists and pilgrims has brought greater attention to the disaster. Dependence on tourism also shapes a community’s capacity to recover. A tragedy of this scale cuts short the tourist season and leaves the people who rely on the trade in a precarious position. There is no magic solution to this, because the other main economic option, agriculture, can be washed away just as thoroughly. Nepal’s National Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Agency estimates that the floods have damaged 1,806 hectares of agricultural land. Rescue operations in Nepal are still under way. The need for emergency relief will persist for months, and recovery could take years. Floods will remain a clear and present danger, and they are likely to become more frequent and more destructive. The crucial question is: What must change to limit the devastation from future floods? As they prepare for that future, Nepal and other places across the Himalayas would benefit from rethinking the type of tourism that they encourage. Nepal is currently a destination for low-cost, mass tourism. Last year, researchers at some of Nepal’s leading environmental NGOs and universities recommended curbing the environmental damage caused by mass tourism without sacrificing its economic benefits.  They proposed a model of smaller-scale, higher-value tourism. Nepal needs fewer visitors who pay more. Bhutan has pursued such a model for decades, carefully controlling visitor numbers and managing tourism’s effects on the country’s environment and society. Costa Rica pursues a similar strategy. Such a transition would not be easy for Nepal. Its infrastructure, businesses and reputation are all geared toward cheap mass tourism. Bhutan’s high-value tourism model benefits from a rarity value: foreign tourists with the exception of Indians have never had easy access to the country. Costa Rica has protected more than a quarter of its landmass in national parks, creating thousands of square miles of protected wilderness for tourists to visit. Other established mass-market destinations, like Thailand, are attempting a similar transition. Nepal could do so, too, but the shift would reduce exposure to disasters only if high-end hotels were built out of harm’s way and the infrastructure supporting them were made resilient. There are also medium term measures that Nepal could adopt, some of them building on work already under way. Training people who live along river basins in evacuation procedures would help them make better use of existing early warning systems. Nepal has a well-established flood warning system, and, on Aug. 26, government authorities sent more than 600,000 early warning text messages to vulnerable communities in the flood’s path within a single minute. A warning is only as useful as the response it evokes. Witnesses say that those who reacted immediately and ran to higher ground survived. Others, who hesitated briefly did not. A teacher who saved 900 students by quickly evacuating his school had answered calls from people he trusted, warning him about flood waters further upstream. In an interview with the BBC, he did not mention receiving an official text alert—a reminder that such warnings are effective only when people trust them and have practiced how to respond. Flood-evacuation training could draw on Nepal’s experience with earthquake preparedness. After the Apr. 25, 2015 earthquake, which destroyed 50,000 classrooms but fortunately struck outside school hours, NGOs and the Nepal government collaborated on a program of drills and training for schoolchildren. That effort offers a potential model for flood preparedness in the mountains. Floods in Nepal will keep coming. Glaciers will melt, rain will fall, and rivers will struggle to carry ever greater volumes of water. Disasters, though, are not inevitable. People will not abandon the Himalayas, and so the task is to find ways of living there more safely. Reducing disaster risk is difficult, but possible. Nepal has already laid much of the groundwork. Now it must build on it.

‘The Law Should Be Shutting Them Down’: Joseph Gordon-Levitt and Others Speak at TIME100 AI Impact Dinner

‘The Law Should Be Shutting Them Down’: Joseph Gordon-Levitt and Others Speak at TIME100 AI Impact Dinner

TIME | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Big AI companies should be “shut down” by the U.S. government until they can prove that their products are safe, actor Joseph Gordon-Levitt said on Monday. In a toast at the third annual TIME100 AI Impact Dinner in San Francisco, the Inception and 500 Days of Summer star told guests that today’s AI makers have lost control of their creations and must be regulated to prevent potential harm. “Why are cars safe to drive? Why are houses safe to live in? It’s because car companies and construction companies ... they have to follow laws. And if they don’t, then the law can come and shut those companies down,” Gordon-Levitt said. “So let’s just be clear here: today’s AI companies are operating in such a way that the law should be shutting them down.” Gordon-Levitt was one of 100 leaders, innovators, storytellers, and activists named in the 2026 TIME100 AI list, which highlights the year’s most influential people in AI. His remarks drew loud clapping, whoops, and cheers from many people in the audience, who gathered at San Francisco’s Asian Art Museum on Monday night to celebrate the 2026 honorees. But Gordon-Levitt’s message was not one of genial praise. He said AI companies’ leaders and employees had publicly admitted their products were dangerous and could not be controlled — a reference, perhaps, to July’s cyberattack by a “swarm” of rogue OpenAI agents as well as multiple recent statements from current and former Anthropic employees warning that AI could end all human life. “To me, that’s pretty obvious that’s the time the law should step in,” Gordon-Levitt said. But, he went on, U.S. politicians had been “timid” and failed to act — perhaps, he alleged, because they were taking money from “some of the guys in this room.” (AI companies, allies, and executives spent at least $83 million on federal elections in 2025 and are set to spend even more this year, according to the New York Times.) He called for Americans not to vote in November's midterms for any politician who stands for "anything less than strong regulation, now." Other toasts highlighted the positive potential of AI, as well as its dangers. The first was by Danielle Boyer, a 25-year-old indigenous American inventor and activist who is using AI to help preserve her people’s endangered language. “My grandmother once told me that no decision belongs only to the moment in which we make it,” Boyer said. “Long after we’re gone, someone we will never meet may still be living with what we chose to do today.” She described her community’s “seven generations” principle: draw inspiration and support from the seven generations before you, and act for the benefit of the seven generations after you. Those teachings inspired her to start building robots to teach Anishinaabemowin, the native language of the Great Lakes region’s Anishinaabe people that was almost wiped out by U.S. residential schools and is now estimated to have fewer than 1,000 fluent speakers. “The decisions we make about artificial intelligence today will outlive every person in this room. That’s crazy, right?” Boyer said. “So let’s build things the right way. Let’s build so that seven generations from now, our descendants can look back at this moment and thank us, rather than have to recover from us.” The final toast came from Suchi Saria, a Johns Hopkins computer scientist and the founder of Bayesian Health who has spent years studying how routinely-collected clinical data and vital signs could help spot an ailing patient before human clinicians notice the problem. “Hospitalized patients can quietly become critically ill from respiratory failure, internal bleeding, kidney failure, dangerous heart rhythms, and many other causes,” she said. Saria saw that firsthand in 2017, when her family in India called to tell her that her nephew was critically ill with sepsis. “We knew the signals were there. We had published the science,” she told the guests on Monday. “And yet, when it mattered to my own family, there was nothing I could offer them. The science had not yet become a system. My nephew died within a few days.” Saria’s voice audibly cracked, and there was a moment of silence. When she spoke again, she described Bayesian Health’s progress on using AI to catch sepsis early — resulting in a 20 percent drop in mortality, the company claims, when clinicians act promptly on the system’s warnings. “The real breakthrough is not prediction. It is not even discovery,” she said. “It is turning insight into action early enough to change what happens to a patient.” The TIME100 Impact Dinner: Leaders Shaping the Future of AI was presented by Deloitte, Booking.com, and Cognizant.

A Night for Actor’s Actors, Not Movie Stars
2026-09-15

A Night for Actor’s Actors, Not Movie Stars

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
When the Widow’s Bay creator Katie Dippold accepted her Emmy for Outstanding Writing for a Comedy Series last night, she joked that she was so proud of her win, she intended to display her trophy as prominently as possible in her home. The award would be mounted on a rotating turntable, she explained; mirrors would be set up around the space so that visitors could always see it, wherever they were. “This is going to become my entire personality now,” she warned. This year’s Emmys essentially turned Widow’s Bay into its personality too. The Apple TV series about a haunted island town full of eccentric inhabitants swept the field, winning six categories over the course of the evening, including Outstanding Comedy Series. The show’s star, Matthew Rhys, even made Emmys history: With his win for Outstanding Lead Actor in a Comedy Series, he became the first-ever nominee to score a trophy in all three lead-actor categories. (Earlier in the night, he was awarded for his work on The Beast in Me, a limited series; in 2018, he won for The Americans, a drama.) His achievements helped underline how much the ceremony honored true-blue actors such as him—and how television, despite years of movie stars dominating prestige shows, remains best designed for versatile performers and textured roles. Consider Rhys’s fellow winners from Widow’s Bay, a series that marries the chill of a Stephen King novel with the goofy humor of Parks and Recreation. Kate O’Flynn, who built her career in British theater, won Outstanding Supporting Actress in a Comedy Series for her spirited turn as Patricia, a lonely but resilient woman. Her co-star Stephen Root, who took home the trophy for Outstanding Supporting Actor in a Comedy Series, is widely regarded as an actor’s actor—the kind of performer who adds depth to any story, no matter the genre. In his speech, he underlined how unusual it was for him to be recognized, pointing out that his son was shocked that he was in the same category as Harrison Ford. The ceremony, of course, featured plenty of movie stars: Michelle Pfeiffer, Nicole Kidman, Tom Holland, and Zendaya were all seated close to the stage. But many of the performers who took home awards were respected TV-industry veterans who have spent most of their career disappearing into their work rather than commanding the spotlight. Noah Wyle may now be an Emmys favorite after winning two years in a row for starring on The Pitt, but he beat out Mark Ruffalo and Gary Oldman for the honor. Allison Janney (who, to be fair, is an Oscar winner) expressed shock about winning her first Emmy in 11 years, for her arresting supporting turn as a fictional U.S. president on The Diplomat. The night also featured several satisfying, overdue wins: Rhea Seehorn, who led six seasons of the often-nominated Better Call Saul, finally took home her first Emmy, for her jittery performance on Pluribus. Hiro Murai, a director who has helped define the cinematic look of modern television through his work on shows such as Atlanta and Barry, won his first directing Emmy, for—once again—Widow’s Bay. The Emmys producers seemed to know that many of its winners might not be household names. The show was front-loaded with the performance categories, which meant that all of the acting awards were finished halfway into the three-hour show. That’s unusual for a Hollywood ceremony, which normally tries to keep people glued to the screen by showing as many stars for as long as possible, and boosting viewer anticipation for their speeches. But then again, the night’s host was Mariska Hargitay, one of television’s mainstays; she’s led the cast of Law & Order: Special Victims Unit for 27 seasons, with another on the way. To honor a slate of experienced pros felt only right.

Jean Smart’s Reminder to the Women in the Room
2026-09-15

Jean Smart’s Reminder to the Women in the Room

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
The actor Jean Smart began her acceptance speech at the Emmys tonight with a request: “Just the men stay standing,” she said to the applauding crowd before her. “The girls get to sit down.” She had just won her fifth-straight award for Outstanding Lead Actress in a Comedy Series for her role on HBO Max’s Hacks, about the tender and testy friendship between an unfiltered comedy legend (played by Smart) and an apprehensive Gen Z comedy writer (Hannah Einbinder). As she clutched her statuette, Smart acknowledged the squeezing and taping and blister-inducing heels that had likely gone into her peers’ glamorous award-show getups. Her well-dressed colleagues in the crowd, she joked, must be muttering, “She’s not that good”—as in, not so talented as to merit a standing ovation that required maneuvering in uncomfortable gowns. Landing that achievement for a character such as Vance feels particularly appropriate. Smart played her as a gutsy, hard-headed, and occasionally vulnerable elder statesman of comedy, who’d erected walls around herself in her fight to be taken seriously in a male-dominated medium. Her win, for a role that embodied the difficulties of being a woman who refuses to placate those around her, parallels her character’s arc; it also calls to mind how tough it is for someone like Vance to earn that level of industry success. Smart, notably, was not the only actor honored this evening for playing an uncompromising woman. Rhea Seehorn, who took home the award for Outstanding Lead Actress in a Drama Series, portrayed the fiercely self-reliant author Carol Sturka on Pluribus, whose stubbornness in a newly dystopian world is laced with grief. Kate O’Flynn won for her supporting role on the comedy series Widow’s Bay as Patricia, a government bureaucrat whose ill-advised attempts to fit in make her a town pariah. And Allison Janney clinched a Supporting Actress trophy for The Diplomat, on which she played U.S. President Grace Penn as a particularly shrewd political operator. Read: The Emmys made Widow’s Bay its personality The actors who played these distinct characters are “linked, not ranked,” as Seehorn said in her speech, quoting the late Gloria Steinem—a nod to the women’s shared lineage as industry colleagues rather than awards competitors. But Smart’s enduring charisma as Vance, and her character’s notable refusal to quit, also enabled her to make this history. In Season 1 of Hacks, much of the humor stemmed from the intergenerational mismatch between Vance and her new joke writer, Ava Daniels, who begin working together at an inopportune time in both of their careers: Daniels has been deemed persona non grata in Hollywood, while Vance is collecting checks from casino gigs in Las Vegas. Both of them eventually manage to find an uneasy rhythm together—but Vance’s professional drive, which often took precedence over the feelings of her closest collaborator, was the show’s engine. Over the course of the next four seasons, Smart revealed Vance’s vulnerable side, even as her character kept up a steady stream of barbed commentary: In her many decades in the industry, Vance had huge opportunities wrenched from her hands—most dramatically her short-lived late-night show, which she spent decades striving for but ultimately abandoned amid studio pressures. But even that move was a testament to the comic’s devotion to her convictions, no matter how unpopular they might make her. By rewarding Smart several times over for this role, the Emmys seemed to recognize something deeper in her performance—and the worth of the woman she so impressively brought to life.

The Supreme Court Recognizes the Danger of Trump’s Election Plan
2026-09-15

The Supreme Court Recognizes the Danger of Trump’s Election Plan

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Neither snow, nor rain, nor heat, nor gloom of night, nor Donald Trump will stop the United States Postal Service from delivering mail-in ballots in the coming midterm elections. Tonight, the Supreme Court handed down an emergency ruling blocking the Trump administration from implementing a new policy that would have made mail-in voting so onerous as to be effectively impossible for election officials to administer. The Court’s decision spares those officials from pointless chaos—and voters from potentially widespread disenfranchisement. In the United States, election administration is carried out by a patchwork of local officials working state by state across roughly 10,000 different jurisdictions. Usually, USPS’s role in elections is clear: The Postal Service transports mail ballots to voters from state and local election offices; if voters return their ballot by mail, USPS takes care of that too. The nature of this responsibility has evolved in recent years, particularly during the coronavirus pandemic, as many states shifted toward encouraging voting by mail. The Trump administration looked at this system, which has been popular among voters, and decided to cause havoc by imposing new and arduous criteria for election officials to meet before USPS would commit to mailing ballots. A number of states and several nonprofits, along with the Democratic Party, quickly brought legal challenges. Before the Court resolved the matter tonight, the cases zagged madly among district and appellate courts, then up to the Supreme Court and back down again. Many judges were skeptical both of the legal basis for the rule and of the speed at which the administration was attempting to implement it, with less than two months to go before the elections. But despite this broad agreement in the lower courts, experts were uncertain how the Supreme Court would handle the case. The Court’s past friendliness toward even the most legally dubious of Trump’s policies cast into doubt whether the justices would stand against his efforts here, especially after the Court rejected an earlier iteration of the challenge on the grounds that it was premature. “The Supreme Court has made law totally unpredictable,” the election-law expert Justin Levitt told me last week, while the country waited for the justices to rule. “In a normal world, there would be a zero percent chance” that the Court would allow the USPS rule to go into effect. Yet, he went on, “we don’t live in a normal world anymore.” Our world is still not a normal one. But this time, a majority of the justices on the Supreme Court decided not to make it worse. In a terse, unsigned paragraph, the Court declared that the Trump administration had failed both to make its case on the law and to show that a ruling in Trump’s favor would not upend the upcoming elections. Justice Brett Kavanaugh, in a brief concurrence, indicated that the Postal Service’s plan might have had firm legal grounding but that he’d agreed to block the policy because “state and local election officials do not have sufficient time to reasonably implement the rule before the elections.” That final part of his assessment is obviously correct. USPS’s plan would have required election officials to upload lists of eligible voters through a Postal Service “portal”—yet the government has given no indication that the portal is operational. USPS would also have required election officials to receive Postal Service approval for the design of their ballot envelopes, but officials have already printed their ballot envelopes and do not have the money or time to create new designs. Last week, a Tallahassee, Florida, paper reported that USPS had rejected the envelope designs submitted by Florida’s Leon County on the grounds that the return address was 0.014 inches too close to other text on the envelope—about the thickness of four sheets of paper. Election administrators prepare to send out ballots far in advance, and if the USPS rule had gone into effect, election officials might have found themselves unable to count on the Postal Service to mail ballots at all. However conservative the Supreme Court is, “there aren’t five votes for abject chaos,” Levitt wrote to me after the ruling. Still, Justices Samuel Alito and Clarence Thomas, the pair on the Court who most reliably tilt toward Trump, would have allowed the USPS plan to go forward. Alito’s dissent is strikingly unconcerned with the potential risks to the midterms if the Postal Service were to refuse to mail ballots, noting only that 12 states (all Republican-led) have “asserted that compliance at this time is practicable.” (Local election officials in some of those states disagreed with that assessment in their own court filings, but Alito does not address this.) Moreover, the federal government’s interest in detecting “election fraud” is compelling, Alito declares. As for the last-minute dash to shake up the midterms, Alito manages to blame not the government but the plaintiffs, the lower courts, and even his own colleagues on the bench for the litigation that—he claims—delayed USPS from finalizing its rule. In a blog post, the UCLA law professor and election scholar Rick Hasen deemed Alito’s dissent “disingenuous.” Among other things, USPS continued working on the rule during the course of the litigation over the summer. And even if the Postal Service had finished the planning process earlier in the summer, state and local jurisdictions still might not have had enough time to rework their envelopes to comply. Fortunately, officials won’t need to scramble to submit envelope designs to USPS to be measured down to fractions of an inch, or upload voter names into a portal that doesn’t exist. Election workers will likely face plenty of challenges in the month and a half before the midterms. Mailing ballots, though, will not be among them.

‘The Flood is the Point’
2026-09-15

‘The Flood is the Point’

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Subscribe here: Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube | Overcast | Pocket Casts Donald Trump loves a deal, even if, at times, it’s just the idea of a deal. The president’s trade dispute with Canada continues to drive one of America’s closest allies into a closer relationship with its chief competition. Although Trump is insisting that “Canada wants to make a deal very badly,” there is very little evidence that’s true, as Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney—enjoying broad national approval for his combative response to the American leader—looks to strengthen its alliances in Europe and Asia. Meanwhile, AI leaders are publicly calling for a slowdown. That was the gist of the message delivered by Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei this weekend in an essay warning that the technological advances of AI are outpacing the ability to ensure that they are safe. His concerns were echoed by some of the biggest names in tech—OpenAI leader Sam Altman and Elon Musk. But the president saw something else in their warnings: “There is a SICK conspiracy going on against AI and Data Centers, and the only one that is happy about it is China,” Trump wrote on his social-media site this morning. This week on Radio Atlantic, my colleagues Ashley Parker and Tom Nichols join me to discuss the president’s trade war with Canada, the Trump-centric Republican midterm convention in Dallas, and whether an aging Congress can regulate AI. The following is a transcript of the episode: Ashley Parker: To be somewhat fair to Trump, or to anyone, is that this is just fundamentally incredibly complex. If you’ve ever watched these members of Congress—which is basically a gerontocracy—try to get to the bottom of Snapchat or Instagram or social media with teens, right, it’s like they’re speaking a foreign language, and those are table stakes, right? AI is a million times more complicated than posting a problematic video on YouTube or Instagram. Adam Harris: This is Radio Atlantic. I’m Adam Harris. Congress is back on Capitol Hill for the last time before the midterm elections, and they have a lot to think about—the burgeoning trade war with Canada and warnings from tech CEOs about the dangers of unregulated AI, among other things. And when those lawmakers return to the campaign trail for the final stretch, the ones in competitive districts may have some company—members of the president’s Cabinet. With me to discuss the news of the week are my colleagues Ashley Parker and Tom Nichols. Let’s get to questions. Harris: Ashley, I want to start with you because you had a story that ran recently about something that people I think have become a little bit desensitized to, and that’s the potential violations of the Hatch Act, which limits the partisan political activities of federal employees. You and our colleague Michael Scherer reported on a competition among the Cabinet secretaries to travel to competitive electoral districts. Can you sort of walk us through that reporting? Parker: Sure. So the first way we heard about this competition, which was not public, was almost inadvertently. We were speaking with a Cabinet secretary who said that there was an event in New York that they had kind of wanted to attend, but had declined because they didn’t want to get docked points in the competition. And we were immediately like, What competition? And so it turns out that there is this unofficial travel competition in the Trump White House. It’s overseen by a woman named Meghan Bauer, who basically coordinates with all of the Cabinet secretaries, officially running between Memorial Day and Labor Day. And basically the goal is to encourage Cabinet officials, Cabinet secretaries to travel the country, particularly to swing districts or swing states that are competitive in the midterms. And each time they go to one of these areas, they get a point. And what this Cabinet official was worried about was losing points, although the official was mistaken. When we began reporting, many officials at the White House told us, Nobody ever loses points; you only accrue points. And they even said, Look, if someone is heading to New York to, say, do a hit on CNN, to talk about Trump’s policies, that still would count as a point. Harris: We can talk about it in point systems, but we should also think of it in terms of the law, right? Like, is this a Hatch Act violation? Parker: So, no, not inherently, but it brings Cabinet officials potentially quite close to the line, and it’s something they need to be careful of. So just to put it in a little historical perspective, this general thing of wanting your top officials, your Cabinet officials, out in the country selling the president’s policies, especially in the run-up to a midterm election, is incredibly common. [George W.] Bush did it; [Barack] Obama did it; [Joe] Biden did it. And Biden, I remember, the most in-demand Cabinet official at the time, when he was president in the run-up to the midterms, was Pete Buttigieg. So Cabinet officials can go and they can talk about—especially if it’s related to their jobs, an agriculture secretary, for instance, or let’s just take Pete Buttigieg for an example. Pete Buttigieg could travel the country and talk about how this stimulus package that Biden and Democrats helped to pass had brought a bridge to your district, had brought new roads to your state, was responsible for huge construction projects over here that had created jobs. That’s all totally fine and not a Hatch Act violation. If it becomes more overtly political—showing up in a district at a campaign stop with, say, a Senate candidate and just saying, Hey, vote for Ken Paxton in Texas—that is where you get into problems. And the Trump officials, some of them have gotten into problems. Harris: Tom, how do you process the sort of flood, the stream of controversy, that comes from the administration? Nichols: Well, I actually am intimately familiar with the Hatch Act because people filed complaints against me constantly when I was a federal employee, before I came to The Atlantic. I was a professor at the Naval War College, which meant I was a Defense Department employee, but I wrote articles and I wrote op-eds and I spoke my mind. And I always finish with, These are my personal views, speaking as an American citizen, et cetera, et cetera. So I think Ashley’s description of, boy, right up to the line, is a great way to think of it. And the larger point, I think, is that, Adam, like you asked: Why does this happen? How do we cope with this flood? And I think that the flood is the point. The fire hose is the point, because at some point, you just sort of throw up your hands and say, Which of these, you know, 9 million things? It used to be that, even in the first Trump term, somebody getting hit with a Hatch Act violation was a big deal. I mean, it made the news. Kellyanne Conway, there were couple of other— Parker: Yeah, I remember the Kellyanne Conway one. And it stood out, right? Nichols: Yeah, right. Because it was rare. And because it was so obvious. And I think it’s part of that whole fire-hosing strategy, where you just sit back and say, Okay, fine. I give up, because tomorrow there will be something else. And then the Hatch Act will seem relatively minor by comparison. Parker: I was thinking about this as I was biking into work today, which is, if you even just look at today’s news, Hatch Act violations are comparatively quaint, right? I mean, I’m biking in, I see that ProPublica has reported that a Russian oligarch who’s very close to [Russian President Vladimir] Putin bankrolled Donald Trump Jr.’s intimate wedding a few months ago. And amid all these warnings that AI is going to kill us in a doomsday scenario, President Trump came out and said, We’re not slowing down; there’s no regulations. So if a Cabinet official says, Hey, vote for this Republican congressman, in the panoply of things that watchdog groups are worrying about, it’s not top of the list, to be frank. Nichols: I know the broken-windows theory of crime has been more or less discredited, but there is a kind of feeling of this of, It starts with the Hatch Act. The Hatch Act is like turnstile jumping in the subway, but enough people do it enough times and pretty soon, you know, it’s people shooting at each other in the streets and robbing banks. And it kind of feels that way now. There are no guardrails. I mean, the story that Ashley just mentioned, about Don Jr.’s wedding. Again, if you were a federal employee, if you even talked to a Russian, that was paperwork right there. I could not imagine saying, Oh yeah, and by the way, he paid for my wedding. But we’ve just kind of gotten used to it. Harris: Republicans gathered in Dallas, Texas, last week for a sort of unusual midterm convention. What did we learn about where Republicans are right now, if anything, coming out of that convention? Nichols: We learned that the party belongs to Donald Trump and that thousands of people will repeat every word he says if he tells them to. That, I think, tells you everything you need to know—about the party, not about the election, but about the party. Parker: I think that’s about right, because a lot of Republicans did not think this was a good use of time or money. If you’re actually focused on winning the midterms, they wanted to be campaigning in their districts, in their states—let alone for some of them, depending on the district or the state, aligning themselves with a president who’s quite unpopular and whose policies are quite unpopular. But nonetheless, you still saw a number of them travel to Dallas despite not really wanting to, because as Tom said, Trump is the leader of this party and he demanded a midterm convention, so he got one. Harris: I was, as I was watching it go on, thinking about what was driving the news as we were heading into the convention, which was this sort of burgeoning trade war with Canada. Trump himself mentioned Canada briefly during the convention, saying— Donald Trump: Canada very much wants to make a deal. They’re dying to make a deal. Harris: Tom, that’s all well and good, but the reality is that the president has chosen to squabble with one of America’s closest allies. Where do things stand now between the U.S. and Canada? And is there any sense of a thawing? Or do you think things are continuing to heat up? Nichols: Well, first, let’s always point out that the president’s default response to any controversy is that Country X is desperate to make a deal. It’s literally Donald Trump’s Mad Lib—one of the Mad Lib sentences. You know, What do you think about the controversy with Country X? The answer is always, Controversy will be over soon. Country X is begging to make a deal. They’re calling me every day, whether it’s Canada, Iran, the Houthis, you name it. I think the Canadians have simply decided they’ve had enough of this nonsense. They are no longer partners; they are adversaries in the economic space. But I think that the Canadians have said, Fine. A majority of the Canadians don’t want to pay for this Trump–Carney tariff war, but they support Carney standing up to Trump. And so my guess is this is not retrievable until Trump is gone from office, which is a real tragedy, even if Trump backs down from the tariffs, which he almost certainly is going to do sooner rather than later, I think. Harris: Yeah. And as you mentioned, right, Carney’s approval rating is quite high. Ashley, how has he been responding to the president, and how has the back-and-forth been going in recent days? Parker: Well, I sort of understand Carney in his response going back to a speech he gave much earlier this year at Davos, where he basically said, The world order as we—I’m paraphrasing here—the world order as we know it is over. And all of these sort of smaller countries have always supplicated themselves to the United States for protection or for deals or for various reasons, and that’s no longer a good strategy for us. And so I think Carney’s response in this trade war is him sort of acting on those principles he laid out at Davos. And one thing that was so striking to me when you look at the back-and-forth—and it looks like both sides had issues; it’s hard to point specifically to which side blew up the most recent deal—but one thing that comes through is Trump has talked about wanting Canada to be a 51st state, and that mainly seems to be trolling, except not entirely; there’s always a kernel of truth with everything Trump does. But in these negotiations, it feels like the American side has sort of internalized the idea that Canada is less a sovereign nation and more a 51st state. And when you look at the terms that Canada objected to, it was stuff the United States negotiators demanded, which would be reasonable if Canada was a state, but is not reasonable if Canada is a sovereign nation and an ally to our north. And it feels like that’s where things fell apart. Harris: Tom, actually in your piece the other day, you talked about the way that the president doesn’t view other nations as sovereign nations. There’s just this idea that he can’t go in and extract things in the way that he would if it were just a part of something that he already owned. Can you sort of flesh out that idea a little bit? Nichols: Well, I mean, not to psychoanalyze the president from a distance, but I think most people would agree the president’s a pretty narcissistic guy. He’s got to put his name on everything. He builds monuments to himself. Every sentence begins with I. And people like that don’t think of other people as real people; they think of them as players on the stage that surrounds them. And I think that’s very clear with Donald Trump. This convention, going back to the convention we were mentioning, you know, he doesn’t really think of other people trying to win elections, and he doesn’t care. They’re supposed to come to his big party and wear a hat and bring noisemakers and that’s it. And that’s the extent of his interest. And I think that’s how he is with foreign countries, that he says they’re all kind of wholly or partially owned subsidiaries of Donald Trump Incorporated. He doesn’t think of them as real places with unique cultures or particular interests led by human beings who may not actually see the world the way he does or care about his interests as much as he cares about his interests. And that’s partly why you always get these kind of stock answers of They’re gonna make a deal, because of course everybody makes a deal, because that’s what I want. And they’re gonna do what I tell them to do, because of course, all my life, everyone has done what I tell them to do. It flummoxes him completely when a foreign nation says, We’re not going to do that. And one of the reasons—you know, I don’t want to extend this analogy by Canada too far—but I think that’s one of the reasons he’s having such a hard time with the war of choice he began in Iran. He just can’t imagine that—he says, I demand that you do X, Y, and Z. And the Iranians say, No and here’s a bunch of missiles and drones as part of that answer. And I think he totally unravels him, because he’s just never had to deal with that. And I think when Mark Carney, you know, as Ashley was saying, Who is this uppity governor, to speak to the president in this way?, instead of the leader of a sovereign nation and place that’s part of NATO and has real interests in the world—one of our second or third biggest trading partners. He cannot internalize that. It doesn’t make it—it’s not like he rejects the idea. It’s like he can’t grasp the idea. Parker: And just to briefly follow on Tom, I mean, Trump has such legitimate faith and confidence in himself as a dealmaker that he really can’t fathom that. You may recall that when he was campaigning, he said, On day one, the war between Russia and Ukraine will end, which is a sort of promise presidents make—day one signals it’s a priority. But in talking to Trump’s aides, they’re like, No, no; he actually thought that. Like, he actually thought that he was such a good dealmaker, it would end. And obviously it hasn’t. Harris: When we come back, we talk about the danger of AI, and Ashley and Tom talk about what they’ll be reporting on in the week ahead. Harris: I wanna switch gears a little bit, speaking of the idea of things that people can and cannot control. Over the last several days, CEOs of leading AI companies have been sounding the alarm, really in a lot of the same ways that Bill Gates, when he was on the show with Hanna [Rosin] not that long ago, was talking about the growing risks of artificial intelligence and the products and the ways that people are using those products. Trump doesn’t appear to be heeding those concerns and has responded that the U.S. has to, quote, “win AI.” What is your reaction to this, to all of this? Nichols: That’s a classic Trump answer. You know, We have to win AI. That’s the president’s way of saying, I didn’t understand the question. I don’t really know what this is, and so we just have to win it. Because that’s a good kind of standard. You could have filled in anything: What do you think about the, you know, international chocolate-milk market? Well, we have to win that. Parker: Yeah, is winning AI, we die first? Nichols: Yeah. (Laughs.) What does that mean? Parker: I will confess, I don’t fully understand all of the intricacies of AI either, but from what I do understand, to be somewhat fair to Trump, or to anyone, is that this is just fundamentally incredibly complex. And, you know, lawmakers, one problem you have is if you’ve ever watched these members of Congress—which is basically a gerontocracy—try to get to the bottom of Snapchat or Instagram or social media with teens, right, it’s like they’re speaking a foreign language. And those are table stakes, right? AI is a million times more complicated than posting a problematic video on YouTube or Instagram. So first of all, a lot of the people in the United States responsible for possibly regulating this are like me, which is terrifying—can’t begin to possibly understand the nuances or the complexities. And the second thing is, even if the United States is willing to regulate, the question is you would sort of need a mutually-assured-destruction pact with China and with other countries. This is a sort of globalized problem. Nichols: I understand the intricacies very well because I have seen all the Terminator movies. And I know that Skynet is going to become self-aware at some point, and we’re going to regret it. But to go back to the president’s response, that would have been a more reassuring answer. So that’s a highly complex thing. We want to make sure that we’re handling it responsibly. And I’m fascinated to know what happened that spooked all of the top, you know, that spooked Altman and Musk and all these other guys. But I’m less of a doomer about it—even if it does scare me. But I think that the first wave of damage, if something like this gets loose, will be economic. But I still don’t know. And, you know, to go back to where we were, I don’t know what it means to win that, other than to say that we will use it to capture more efficiencies than other countries will and put more people out of work faster or something. But I think the danger of this thing is real because the people handling it—I just don’t have a sense that they are looking at this with the kind of civic responsibility that they should have. Harris: Ashley and Tom, I’m going to ask you to open your notebooks. What questions do you have going into this week? And, Ashley, I’m going to go to you first. Parker: Sure. So I’m gonna be looking at—I think actually last time I was on your podcast, we were talking about the midterms and why the bottom hasn’t fallen out for the Republicans yet. And, you know, that that was August, but things really kick off after Labor Day. There’s October surprises; voters tune in. I mean, one thing that was fascinating is I talked to someone who does focus groups with swing-state voters who said, You hold up a picture of the Senate candidate in a state, and half the voters don’t even know who he or she is. So I’m still gonna be asking those similar midterm questions, but at a time when everyone is tuning in and ads land harder; mistakes, gaffs land harder; trying to see where this heads in November. Harris: And the Republicans are starting to put more of their money into their advertising now. They’re sort of using that war chest we were talking about the last time. Parker: Yeah, because again, part of this is: They would have liked to have used it earlier. Trump has a lot of money; his groups have a lot of money. But he had to kind of release it. And even if they didn’t spend it, they wanted to get those TV reservations up early because you can get a cheaper rate if you book early. But the other reason you spend now is because this is when people are paying attention. And Michael Scherer and I spoke to Trump’s pollster Tony Fabrizio, who said at the time when we spoke, he said, Look, yeah, things are bad for the Republicans, but that’s because we haven’t turned to the Democrats yet. The Democrats have a horrible brand, and you should expect to see a ton of contrast ads because elections, as Biden always says, and as everyone understands, They’re a choice. Nichols: Ashley mentioned, or Adam, I think you mentioned October surprises. And I’m looking for one because when Trump—it gets panicky. I think one of the things I saw at the convention was that he felt very ebullient and energized, but also there was a lot of fear there. He clearly is deeply worried. And when he gets like that, you’re gonna get a surprise of some kind. He’s gonna. And it’s making me wonder what kind of thing will pop. So I guess I’m worried politically about a black-swan event, where he just does something to change the discussion. The other thing I’m watching, though, is that both in Russia and Iran, these situations are not going away. They are aware that there’s an election impending. And I have to say I don’t know why this is, but late September, early October is always a very fertile time for an international crisis. And I’m concerned that that crisis could erupt either in the Pacific, in Russia, or in Iran, because they’re not on our timetable. They’re not gonna do things on Donald Trump’s timetable or do things based on his interests. And I think all of those places that we’ve stopped watching temporarily because of so much going on at home are percolating, and I’m concerned about what happens next in each of those three, especially in the Ukraine war and in Iran. Harris: Well, Ashley, Tom, thank you so much for joining me today and helping me sift through the news of the week. The Atlantic
Trump’s See-No-Evil AI Policy
2026-09-15

Trump’s See-No-Evil AI Policy

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Not long ago, most of Washington was scrambling to be as friendly as possible to the rise of artificial intelligence. Lawmakers from both parties saw a chance to bolster the nation’s economy—and their campaign coffers. But rarely has public opinion flipped so quickly and so thoroughly as it has on AI in recent months. While AI was making some tech leaders unfathomably rich, many Americans in states both red and blue grew alarmed by the proliferation of loud, electricity-guzzling data centers, convinced that AI would first make life more expensive and then, for good measure, eliminate their job. In the past few days, some of the top voices in the AI industry have gone further, warning that unless development of the technology is slowed, and slowed soon, all of humanity could be made extinct. These fears have rattled markets and galvanized a bipartisan set of leaders into calling for the government to step in. AI has now become a central issue in the midterm elections. But President Trump, who first came to power by closely reading the mood of the country and shifting his views to meet those of the electorate, seems to have his fingers in his ears, ignoring it all and continuing to embrace AI, confident that everything will be fine because he is in charge. Unless, of course, the human race ends first. Trump would seem to have a perfect opportunity to try to contain this fast-moving political conflagration: Next week, he’s set to host China’s president, Xi Jinping, at the White House. The United States and China are the world’s leaders on AI and are in a race to develop the technology. The summit could be a venue, some in the industry hope, to order a pause in development or establish international safety guidelines. But Trump, at least for now, wants no part of a slowdown. AI growth has propped up Wall Street and sectors of the American economy for the duration of his second term, and the president has made clear that he doesn’t want to do anything that could burst that bubble. “The only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!” Trump wrote on Truth Social today, as he made half a dozen posts, each seemingly more frantic than the last. He later compared the concerns about the technology to his own impeachments, declaring that “AI taking over the World, destroying Humanity, and all other things bad, is a HOAX.” As tech insiders offered warnings about the AI apocalypse, Trump was busy rallying the MAGA faithful in Texas and taking in rounds of golf in Ireland. The dire prophecies began last Tuesday, with a series of viral social-media posts by an AI researcher who said that the technology is advancing so quickly that it could bring about human extinction by the end of the decade. The threat of annihilation seemed to grab Washington’s attention as few other concerns have. By the next afternoon, after several other researchers had echoed the alarm, members of Congress were beginning to call for legislation, new agencies, hearings, and regulations to address the threat. Senator Bernie Sanders of Vermont told us on Wednesday that he had sent invitations to a bipartisan set of lawmakers for a closed-door briefing with AI experts. He said that the researchers had provided a wake-up call about a technology that poses an “extraordinary danger for the human race.” That night, Trump spoke for nearly two hours at the Republican midterm convention in Dallas, but he did not mention AI once. Read: AI is running laps around our political system In the days that followed, more troubling evidence emerged. Anthropic released a report detailing the ways that individuals and nation-states have attempted to misuse its technology, including for sophisticated cyberattacks and research that could potentially support the development of biological weapons. Trump decamped to Ireland, where he spent the weekend watching a golf tournament at one of his properties. He was dismissive of the Silicon Valley warnings, emphasizing the need to defeat China in the AI race and downplaying the technology’s risks. On Saturday, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei wrote that a downshift in the pace of AI development “could greatly reduce the risk that something goes seriously wrong.” Other industry leaders joined in, including OpenAI’s Sam Altman and Elon Musk, the sometimes–Trump ally. One of Trump’s first moves upon returning to office last year was to revoke the executive order signed by his predecessor, Joe Biden, that imposed a series of regulations on the AI industry. Trump’s focus was business growth, and he reveled in the power he had over the nation’s tech titans. Many crowded the room at his inauguration, and he has hosted AI-industry leaders such as Altman, former Oracle CEO Safra Catz, and Nvidia President Jensen Huang. Still, some of Trump’s advisers have had concerns since last year about the political impacts of the AI backlash, when figures as disparate as Sanders and Florida Governor Ron DeSantis began calling for more regulation. To head off the potential populist backlash, Trump announced the Ratepayer Protection Pledge in March, asking major developers of AI to “build, bring, or buy” resources needed for any new electricity-production and utility-infrastructure upgrades. Now the White House is working with House Speaker Mike Johnson to fast-track legislation that would put into law some of the pledge’s voluntary measures. A vote is scheduled for tomorrow. From the March issue: America isn’t ready for what AI will do to jobs People close to the administration perceive some figures, including White House Chief of Staff Susie Wiles and Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, to be supportive of additional regulation of the industry, two people familiar with internal White House deliberations told us, speaking on the condition of anonymity. Others, such as Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, have advocated for as little government interference as possible, while Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth has demanded that Anthropic abandon ethical safeguards—such as rules against mass surveillance and autonomous lethal targeting—in its work for the Pentagon. (When Anthropic refused, Hegseth cut ties with the company.) Trump has dug in on letting AI rip. He has watched as the industry has propelled the stock market, the president’s favorite indicator of a strong economy, keeping it high even amid challenges posed by the Iran conflict, stubborn inflation, and tariffs. Trump has expressed worry to those close to him that a halt to AI expansion could send the economy into a recession, one ally told us. Some in the president’s orbit have raised suspicions about the AI executives’ warnings: Is this an attempt at market capture? (They worry that new regulations would freeze the industry and prevent would-be competitors from catching up to the industry giants.) Vice President Vance seemed to give expression to that idea by saying today that tech companies “begging the government to regulate them” feels “like a bit of a Trojan horse.” David Sacks, a co-chair of the President’s Council of Advisors on Science and Technology, wondered on social media over the weekend why AI companies cannot just regulate themselves; he told tech leaders to “stop pretending you need anyone else’s permission. Stop pretending antitrust law has to be suspended so you can form a cartel.” (Top AI firms could work together to implement some safety standards, but antitrust law would likely prevent them from implementing any kind of reciprocal agreement to throttle the pace of development, unless Congress granted a waiver for that purpose, MacKenzie Arnold, the managing director of the Institute for Law and AI, a nonpartisan think tank, told The Atlantic.) Today, Trump called Huang while the CEO was onstage at an AI event to assure the Nvidia head that he will keep supporting the technology. The pleas for action have created unlikely political bedfellows. Senator Ted Cruz of Texas, a Republican, declared that “we need substantially more regulation,” while Senator Brian Schatz of Hawaii, a Democrat, said that Congress needs to “move at the pace of the threat.” Tomorrow, Sanders will join the Trump adviser Steve Bannon at a “Pro-Human Assembly,” an all-day gathering in Washington, D.C., focused on ensuring that humans remain in charge of AI. Despite support for regulation from both the right and the left, there is little meaningful oversight of AI that can be implemented without the president’s approval. Sanders told us that he hopes the bipartisan outrage will ultimately get Trump’s attention. “Trump’s ignorance is extremely dangerous,” he said. “He has not a clue about the dangers inherent in this technology, nor do people in his administration.” Trump is looking forward to next week’s summit with Xi, which follows a May meeting in Beijing. The U.S. president has stressed his strong relationship with the Chinese leader and has downplayed China’s efforts to thwart the United States. It seems unlikely that Trump will let AI fears fully derail the summit’s agenda. Trump has also minimized China’s chances of catching up to the U.S.—he green-lighted a sale of Nvidia chips to China late last year—even as he has played up the stakes. “Whoever wins AI wins,” he said this past weekend. But Xi may come into the meeting with a new wariness of his own. China’s top intelligence official warned yesterday that AI could pose a direct threat to the Chinese Communist Party’s hold on power by enabling sophisticated hacking and disinformation campaigns. Many in the industry hope that the summit will be an inflection point. Nate Persily, a co-director of the Stanford Law AI Initiative, told us that “if ever there was an opportunity to get some kind of consensus on AI safety, this provides that opportunity.” And Altman, in an interview over the weekend, made a clever appeal to Trump’s vanity, suggesting that if he and Xi strike some sort of deal to create AI guardrails, the president could win his long-coveted Nobel Peace Prize. Michael Scherer, Ashley Parker, Will Oremus, and Jack Rodriquez-Vars contributed reporting.
The Atlantic
How Long Can the U.S. Escape an Atlantic Hurricane?
2026-09-14

How Long Can the U.S. Escape an Atlantic Hurricane?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. Every president likes to take credit for good news on their watch, but most of them have at least a little shame about it. Not Donald Trump. September 10 is typically the peak of Atlantic Ocean hurricane season. The National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration expected a relatively restrained year, but as the meteorologist and hurricane specialist Michael Lowry writes, the season “has never gone beyond September 11th in the reliable records (since at least 1950) without a hurricane” in the Atlantic Ocean—until this year. There aren’t even any strong prospects forming. Amid spreading violence in the Middle East, warnings of an AI apocalypse, and high fuel prices, it’s a rare piece of good news, and Trump wants credit. Over the weekend, the White House flagged the news, commenting, “Thank you, President Trump!” This is absurd, though maybe no one should be surprised that a man who believed that marking a map with Sharpie can change a hurricane’s path would take credit for a low-key storm season. (He also reportedly once suggested attacking storms with nuclear weapons, so perhaps, as the journalist Catherine Rampell jokes, this is deterrence in action.) Better explanations than presidential benevolence exist. As my colleague Joshua Partlow has reported, this is an unusual year for the El Niño cycle. In the Pacific Ocean, it has produced very warm water, as well as a number of hurricanes. Hawaii, which is seldom hit, has been struck by two hurricanes so far this year. At least four deaths have been attributed to the storms, and power remains out on some parts of the island of Kauai. But scientists say that El Niño also accounts for the absence of major storms, so far, in the Atlantic. “It’s really staggering, just how inhospitable the Atlantic is to anything that could form a tropical cyclone right now,” Kristen Corbosiero, a professor of atmospheric science at the University at Albany, told PBS. The lull is especially notable because last year’s hurricane season was also astonishingly mild in the United States. Although parts of the Caribbean endured hurricanes, not a single one hit the U.S. mainland. This is great news for the Americans who are otherwise often in hurricanes’ paths, and who have been slammed by a series of storms in the past decade—including 2024’s Hurricane Helene, the deadliest to hit the mainland United States since Hurricane Katrina, in 2005. It is also great news for Trump, who must be one of the luckiest people in history. Hurricanes can help or, more often, badly hurt a president. George W. Bush’s reputation never recovered from Katrina, whereas Barack Obama’s steady hand during Hurricane Sandy seemed to bolster his reelection campaign. Trump’s handling of storms during his first term was poor; he demonstrated little human warmth and, in Puerto Rico after Hurricane Maria, jarring coldness. Trump’s personal behavior aside, his administration has been especially fortunate because under his leadership, FEMA appears to be in no shape to handle a major disaster. In the first months of his second presidency, Trump and then–Secretary of Homeland Security Kristi Noem announced plans to dismantle FEMA entirely, devolving responsibility for relief to states, an idea panned by most experts. In the meantime, he seemed determined to slowly strangle it, appointing interim leaders with no disaster-relief experience. First came Cameron Hamilton, who was fired after telling Congress that he did not support liquidating FEMA. Next was David Richardson, who, shortly after taking his post in May 2025, acknowledged that he had no completed plan for the impending hurricane season and would reuse the previous year’s. (FEMA employees reported that Richardson said he didn’t even realize such a season existed; a FEMA spokesperson later said he was joking.) Because no storms have since struck the mainland, these failures ended up having much less human and economic cost. FEMA appears to be on a somewhat stronger footing now. Trump has backed off plans to eliminate FEMA, Noem and Richardson are gone, and Hamilton is back as permanent administrator—and has won some tentative praise for working to learn more about disaster relief. He inherits serious challenges, though. A federal judge ruled on Friday that an administration order to lay off half of FEMA’s workforce was illegal. “Frankly, the FEMA staffing plan number appears as if pulled from thin air,” the judge wrote of the government’s proposal, which included cutting thousands of on-call employees who rush in with aid after a disaster. A report released last month by the Government Accountability Office was even more scathing. The GAO found that FEMA’s personnel decisions had been made “without assessing whether it had the necessary staffing capacity to meet its mission,” including what it is required to do under law, and that “FEMA is not positioned to determine whether it has the right number of people with the right skills in the right positions at the right time to meet its mission.” Furthermore, the report said, “the agency has no overall strategic direction.” Alas, the calm before the storms won’t last indefinitely. Experts who spoke with The New York Times said that it would be premature to assume that no major Atlantic hurricanes will form this year. If the president’s political luck continues, then perhaps the East Coast won’t see a really bad storm until after the midterm elections, or maybe even next year. Given his administration’s approach to hurricane preparedness and the travails of FEMA, though, ordinary Americans along the Atlantic coast and the Gulf Coast are likely to see their weather luck run out. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: The Biggest Obstacle to Universal Child Care: Babies Last year seemed like a pretty big one for breakthroughs in child care. In New York City, Zohran Mamdani’s promise of publicly funded care for young kids helped him win the keys to Gracie Mansion. Officials in Connecticut created an endowment to make care more affordable in that state. And New Mexico became the first state to commit to free child care for all of its residents. But as policy makers have begun hammering out the details of these ambitions, they’ve hit against a stubborn and formidable obstacle: babies. Take a closer look. Before the photographer Edward Steichen left painting behind forever, he attempted to refocus it on nature, Susan Tallman writes. In a galaxy far, far away. The Star Wars creator George Lucas’s next great epic is an art museum, James Parker writes. Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. When you buy a book using a link in this newsletter, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

I don't know about any particular phone, but in many devices the long press power off is an old-fashioned kill switch that charges a capacitor, eventually reaching the voltage to turn off a transistor connected to a "power good" line on the power management IC. And inside the IC it's the same deal. So as long as the button works, it should be able to hard power off, even if the software is badly bugged. Actually numerous times I've seen people complaining their phone or tablet wouldn't turn off, when actually the software was just frozen and they didn't know about long press power off. "May I see that?" "Wow... that's amazing!" "To you, maybe..." Not necessarily a capacitor; sometimes a little counter circuit, on the MCU but independent of the CPU and not controllable (or vetoable) by it. In either case, not prone to systemic failures of the higher-level logic. People overestimate the difficulty of adding low-level contingencies to high-level systems, and underestimate engineers' eagerness to do so. The Luna experiment illustrates an important distinction between task automation and organizational autonomy. An agent may be capable of interacting with email, browsers, telephones, financial systems, and other operational tools, yet still lack the higher-level judgment required to operate a viable business. Retail, for example, requires continuous decisions about demand, inventory, pricing, customer behavior, risk, and capital allocation. These are not simply interface problems. The vending-machine incident demonstrates a related issue: increasing an agent's operational authority also increases the potential consequences of poorly specified objectives, adversarial inputs, and unforeseen edge cases. Giving an autonomous system access to financial resources therefore changes the problem from "Can the agent perform the task?" to "Can the agent reliably determine which actions should be performed?" That makes Pion an interesting research direction. A platform that enables many independent experiments could provide considerably more evidence about the practical limits of autonomous agents than a small number of carefully controlled demonstrations. The ultimate measure of success, however, should not be whether an agent can technically operate a company. It should be whether the resulting organization can make sound decisions, remain economically viable, respond appropriately to unexpected conditions, and produce value for actual customers. In other words, autonomy is not merely the ability to act without a human in the loop. It is the ability to exercise useful judgment when the correct action is not already specified. No Rolling Power Outages for California Since 2020 - Thanks to 17,000 MW of New Battery Storage
2026-09-14

No Rolling Power Outages for California Since 2020 - Thanks to 17,000 MW of New Battery Storage

Slashdot | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Summer loads typically peak mid-day and follow solar generation closely... batteries help make managing supply and demand easier, but huge capacity isn't necessarily needed or helpful. By contrast, it's winter loads that experience the morning/evening "double peak". That's where these large capacities come into play... the sun directly warms houses during the middle of the day (cutting the need for heating) and the batteries soak up excess dollar generation, which then gets spent as the sun sets and in the morning hours the next day. 1. Don’t expect those prices to last. Distribution costs are rising everywhere and really sharply. Go watch a Mr Global video on this2. Californians earn more. The 10 states whose electricity is least affordable (ie costs compared to incomes) are all red states > Chinese wind mills and solar panels are great although and extremely good for the environment. The wild life loves them, especially the birds. Wind mills, 350 thousand dead birdsHydro lines, 35 million deadBuildings, 950 millionHouse cats, 4 billion https://climate.mit.edu/ask-mi... You're right the article is light on details. They did install 17,000 MW worth of batteries; ~13,500 was grid-scale storage and the rest was residential supported through rebates. The rebates on the batteries you earn over time, and to do so you must export some of your stored battery power back to the grid during peak hours. So about 15-20% was residential people incentivized to use their home batteries as storage and the rest was grid storage. The article doesn't point out that California also added 27,000 MW of generation capacity also but good news it was almost entirely renewables, wind and solar mostly; natural gas power generation has been flat in it's power generation capacity. They're typically Lithium Iron Phosphate (LFP), good for 5000+ cycles before they degrade enough to be a problem. So charging every day they're good for about 10 to 15 years. In practice, they'll have a site with multiple generations of batteries. So they're always extending their capacity and keeping old batteries in service. The Supreme Court’s Conservatives and Liberals Just Came to the Brink—and Finally Said “No”
2026-09-15

The Supreme Court’s Conservatives and Liberals Just Came to the Brink—and Finally Said “No”

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. There are two ways of looking at the Supreme Court’s three-sentence ruling in USPS v. California Monday evening that put an end to Donald Trump’s attempt to disenfranchise millions of elderly, disabled, military, rural, Native American and other voters in the 2026 midterm elections who depend upon vote by mail as their only viable option to cast a ballot. The first is to lament that the government pushed this case to the limits—going not once or twice, but three times to the Supreme Court—and that two Justices, Samuel Alito and Clarence Thomas were willing to simply ignore mass disenfranchisement and side with Donald Trump’s bogus voter fraud crusade. The second is to note that the court’s conservatives and liberals came to the brink, saw that ruling the other way risked serious election subversion, and said “No.” For the time being, let’s celebrate that the center of the court did hold, even as dangers remain ahead in both 2026 and 2028. Slate readers are already familiar with this long saga, so just a very brief review is necessary. Back in March, President Trump put out an executive order on voting (his second since returning to office in 2025) that among other things directed the post office set up a new rule imposing a set of requirements for states that wanted to continue to have their ballots delivered by mail. USPS’s rule issued in late August required that states get USPS approval for new envelope designs with intelligent bar codes on them, to put those bar codes on each envelope, upload a list of the state’s voters to a USPS portal, and to submit the ballots for the post office to check the names encoded in the bar codes against the voter list. The rule further required the USPS to reject an entire batch of ballots if there was one potential mismatch. In the brief moment when courts did not block the rule from going into effect, USPS rejected Leon County, Florida’s ballot design because its required printed notation about return mail was 0.014 of an inch too close to other information on the envelope. Now complying with all these new rules sounds pretty hard for election administrators to do with lead time and practice. But the government tried to impose these rules for the already ongoing midterm elections. Envelopes have already been loaded with ballots and addressed. Indeed, North Carolina began mailing ballots on September 4, and other states are doing so now. Even as of Monday’s Supreme Court’s ruling, USPS did not have a working portal for states to upload their voter lists, not even on a voluntary basis as USPS had said would be possible by last week. The upshot of USPS’s administrative failure is that the rule would have stopped the agency from accepting ballots from all states because there could be no matching between the envelope and the portal. That means that the most vulnerable voters who depend on vote by mail around the country would have been disenfranchised, including core Republican constituencies. The federal district court issued a preliminary injunction partly on this basis, noting the burdens on election administrators were high and the public interest costs dire. The district court also found that the rule exceeded USPS’s authority to issue. The Supreme Court’s terse order agreed on both points. It held that the government was likely to lose on the legal question of USPS’s authority. And it held that the “equitable factors”—that is, the burden on election administrators and the voting public—outweighed the government’s purported interest in stopping voter fraud. White hot fury that the government tried so hard to get this rule in place for the 2026 elections, knowing full well the consequences had it been successful at the Supreme Court, is the correct emotion. A good legal brief takes on the strongest argument of the other side and deals with it head-on. In this case, the government had to overcome the district court’s factual findings about the unfairness of the rule. But the government’s brief in this case simply ignored the mass disenfranchisement point, one flagged by the states and voting rights organization plaintiffs as well as in a professors’ amicus brief that the Safeguarding Democracy Project filed in this case. The failure to address the mass disenfranchisement point essentially conceded it, and that was enough for the court majority. But it was not enough for Justice Samuel Alito, dissenting along with Justice Clarence Thomas. Alito first concluded that the U.S. was likely to win on the merits. When he turned to the equities, he parroted the empty and unsupported claim that the USPS rules would stop fraud. On the other side of the ledger, he said that states were divided on the equities because some Trump-supporting state attorneys general filed a brief siding with the government; never mind that some of those states’ chief election officers filed on the other side. In any case, he went on to blame the states for litigating the case so hard that it somehow stopped the government from getting its rule and portal up and running, and he once again implicitly blamed Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson for slow-walking the case when it first came before the justices in July. Totally absent from Justice Alito’s balancing were the voters. They weren’t an afterthought; their interests were not considered at all. This is unsurprising, coming from the justice who has done more to hurt the cause of voting and voting rights than any on the court in at least a century. So there are good reasons to be furious, and worried about what this portends for the future. But let’s not be all gloom and doom. We know that Justice Kavanaugh, who agreed in a separate concurrence that the government might win on the merits, saw that the equities favored the voters this time around. And we can be sure that at least two of the court’s other conservatives agreed too, along with the court’s three liberals on both the merits and the equities. A third conservative justice either agreed with the majority or did not see fit to side with either Alito’s dissent or Kavanaugh’s concurrence. That ain’t nothing. Just as in the period after the 2020 election, when Donald Trump tried to subvert the outcome of the presidential election with bogus legal arguments and conspiracy theories, the Supreme Court again stood firm and said “No.” Back then, it rejected without comment an original petition from Texas’s Attorney General Ken Paxton to throw out the results from some states that voted for President Joe Biden. Now, it failed to countenance a mass disenfranchisement just weeks before a critical midterm. The Supreme Court has been terrible on voting rights, but so far it has held the line on actual election rigging. Let’s celebrate that, even as we gird for more battles to come.
